The compiler's module cache holds the templates but not their instantiations, and each shader is a new `metal` process, so it instantiates them again, along with a `memory_coherence_device` copy of each it uses: 722 of a typical shader's 902 ms of front end by `-ftime-trace`, against about 180 ms for the shader's own text. Declaring the argument buffer alone took 0.37 s of that shader's compile.
- **Fix:** `dm/gfx/tools/dmsh_pch.py` makes one precompiled header per compile convention at a process's first compile (`dmsh_build.py` asks for them before it forks its workers), holding those instantiations, and `dmsh_build.py` and `ios_metal.py` compile with `-include-pch`. The header adds only the two includes the sources start with and declares no name, and a class left out of it costs only time. The output is byte-identical, so the compile records and the Metal compile cache's keys stay as they were. `make` checks this on a test shader and builds without the header if a compiler differs, and a compile that fails with the header runs again without it, so a failure reports what a plain compile reports. `DMSH_PCH=0` turns it off.
- **Measurement:** cold builds of 1,019 Pokopia shaders on an M4 Pro (`J=10`, with other jobs on the Mac): Mac child processes 2,343 to 975 s of CPU, wall 253–263 s to 176–182 s; iOS child processes 981–1,077 to 574–605 s, wall 179–233 s to 130–153 s. At that rate the whole Mac map would take about 6.4 hours instead of about 9 (not run). All 37,611 Mac files of 8,157 Pokopia shaders and all 3,675 iOS files of 1,020 came out byte-identical with and without the header, as did 1,600 compiles of eight games' shaders.
- **Open:** wall time fell less than CPU, as the builder's Python took about 0.12 s a shader under the GIL ([Rewrites that try every character of a large source](#rewrites-that-try-every-character-of-a-large-source) lowered that).
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Pokopia](../../dm/games/0400A3A01EE4C000/README.md#found-on-this-game).
- **Check:** `test_shader_pch.py`.

### Rewrites that try every character of a large source

- **Symptom:** the shader step of a game with large translated sources has fewer Metal compiles running than `J` asks for, and `dmsh_build.py`'s own process stays busy on one core between them. Pokémon Pokopia's sources average 220 KB, and the builder spent about 0.12 s of Python on each shader, all but its system calls under the GIL, which alone would have come to about 4.2 hours of its full build.
- **Cause:** patterns with no literal start, which `re` can only try at every character: `_once` in `dmsh_fetch.py` began with a lookbehind and ran 24 times a pixel shader at about 4 ms (38% of the Python, on shaders that mostly make no variant), the attribute pattern ran on every shader though only vertex shaders have attributes, and patterns beginning `\b` or an alternation did the same in `reflect`, `fs_uint` and `check_msl`. `dmsh_side.py` `_close` looped in Python over each character of a function body, and `uses_image_atomics` over each SPIR-V instruction.
- **Fix:** the same questions, answered from the text: `str.find` and a look at the character before it (`_once`, `has_word`), one pass over a shader's stores instead of one for each output (`fs_uint`), the lines that hold attributes (`attributes`), bracket to bracket (`_close`), and a test for a word every match must hold before the pattern runs (`check_msl`, `uses_image_atomics`). The outputs are byte-identical.
- **Measurement:** cold builds on an M4 Pro, old and new taking turns, never on a quiet Mac. Pokopia's 8,157 `shader_seen.tsv` shaders, Mac and iOS (`J=14`, load 9 to 19): wall 1,426 to 607 s, the builder's Python 1,121 to 463 s of CPU. 1,001 of them, Mac only: `J=14` at load 5 to 8, wall 141 to 68 s, Python 116 to 52 s; `J=14` at load 26 to 39, wall 194 to 94 s; `J=10` at load 31 to 64, wall 177 and 173 s to 92 and 93 s, Python 150 to 160 s to about 49 s. The child processes' CPU stayed about the same (4,842 to 4,789 s on the 8,157; 2 to 13% more on the 1,001, with more compiles running at once). At those rates the whole Mac map at the NSP script's `J=14` would take about 2.5 hours instead of about 5 (not run). Identical: all 31,836 Mac and 23,678 iOS files of those 8,157, and `regress.py chain` on all of Breath of the Wild's 19,592 shaders (0 changed); old against new, step by step, 0 differences on 27,325 sources of seven games, 8,197 of their fetch variants among them.
- **Open:** a pool of worker processes for the finish step gained only 6 to 8% more on top of this here (1,001 shaders, `J=10` and `J=14`, also with half the compiles modelled) and was not built then; on Macs with more cores it gained much more, and the build now has one ([A shader build's Python finishes one shader at a time](#a-shader-builds-python-finishes-one-shader-at-a-time)). `posix_spawn` (`close_fds=False`) takes `Popen` from 13 to 3 s, but without `pipe2` on macOS another thread's child can inherit a pipe and hold up its reader, so it would need a lock around every launch. The reflections a full build keeps (about 2.5 GB for Pokopia) raise the cost of each fork (of each child the build launches; its worker processes are forked once, before the first result); a micro-benchmark gives 0.7 ms at 176 MB and 1.8 ms at 2 GB, and no full map has been timed.
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Pokopia](../../dm/games/0400A3A01EE4C000/README.md#found-on-this-game) (resolved).
- **Check:** `test_shader_rewrites.py`, which also holds each replacement to the pattern or loop it replaced on random text.

### A shader build's Python finishes one shader at a time

- **Symptom:** a cold shader step leaves about half the cores idle whatever `J` is, and `dmsh_build.py`'s own process stays at one full core from start to end. Ocarina of Time's whole Mac map, 42,848 shaders: 3,506 s on an M3 Max with 16 cores (`J=16`, the build at 104% CPU, 4 to 5 cores busy in all), 3,002 s on an M5 Max with 18 cores.
- **Cause:** two. Finishing a shader (the MSL rewrites, its reflection, its outputs and the cache) is Python, about 40 ms a shader on Ocarina of Time, and the threads that did it share one GIL, so one core finished every shader while the Metal compiles the threads waited on left the others idle ([Rewrites that try every character of a large source](#rewrites-that-try-every-character-of-a-large-source) lowered that cost; free-threaded Python 3.14t was slower, not faster). And the shaders went in batches of `4 × J`: the next batch started when the slowest shader of this one was done, with every other thread idle.
- **Fix:** `dmsh_build.py` finishes the shaders in `DMSH_PROCS` worker processes (default `J`; 0 finishes them in threads, as before). The workers are forks of the build, made once, after the cache check and before its first thread, so they have every table and fingerprint it read; spawned workers would run the script again, and its start clears the outputs. No worker opens `cas.db`: the build makes their cache calls on its one connection ([A forked shader worker dies opening the compile cache](#a-forked-shader-worker-dies-opening-the-compile-cache); as first written each opened its own). The precompiled Metal headers are made before the fork, or each worker would make its own. The build still translates the batches itself and hands a worker each shader's job; the worker's result and counts come back and are added in batch order, so the summary, the progress file and every output are those of the threads. The next batch is handed out before this one is done (two at most), so no worker waits for a batch's slowest shader; paired vertex shaders still wait for every other shader (`vs_pad` reads their pixel shaders' MSL). A worker that dies stops the build with one `[RED]` line. The outputs are byte-identical; the change is to the chain's code, so it changes the chain version once.
- **Measurement:** cold builds, empty cache, each against the main it was written on.
  - Ocarina of Time's whole Mac map on the M3 Max (`J=16`): 3,506 to 1,826 s; with every shader restored from the cache 888 to 191 s. Every 8th key (9,133 shaders): 544 to 292 s, 6.4 to 13.4 cores busy; 417 s in threads with the batch overlap alone; 403, 400 and 388 s with 12, 16 and 20 workers.
  - An M6 with 12 cores and 16 GB (`J=12`, another game running beside it). Red Dead Redemption's 3,628 shaders: Mac 54 to 35 s, Mac and iOS 84 s to 53 and 63 s; with every shader restored from the cache 11 to 13 s (the headers are made though nothing compiles). 1,920 of Metroid Prime 4: Beyond's shaders, whose compiles are much longer: 105 to 72 s, and 89 s with `DMSH_PROCS=6`.
  - Memory, the summed resident size of the build and everything it runs, sampled every 0.5 s on the M6 (it counts the pages the forks share once for each): Red Dead Redemption 0.45 to 0.8 GB, the Metroid Prime 4 shaders 1.1 to 3.4 GB, and 2.0 GB with `DMSH_PROCS=6`. A worker itself held about 35 MB; the rest is `J` compilers running at once, which the threads never reached. With a header made in each worker, as first written, Red Dead Redemption's build peaked at 1.4 GB.
  - Identical apart from the chain version and `_b_cas.txt`: all 173,316 files of Ocarina of Time's map, and Red Dead Redemption's Mac and iOS libraries (15,542 and 11,912 files).
  - Another design, measured and not taken ([PR #2035](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/2035)): the build as `J / 3` copies of itself, each with a share of the shaders. Ocarina of Time's map on the M5 Max 3,002 to 1,298 s, and 1.41 times faster on an M3 Pro with 11 cores for 1.3 GB more; on the M6 it gained less than the forked workers (Red Dead Redemption: Mac 75 to 58 s, Mac and iOS 104 to 95 s, against its own older main) and compiled about 9% of the Metal sources twice.
- **Open:** not measured on a Mac with 8 GB, where `DMSH_PROCS` below `J` trades the gain for memory, nor a whole large map with iOS libraries. Two workers can compile the same Metal source at once (about 10 of Red Dead Redemption's 2,159 compiles), which costs time only.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md#found-on-this-game).
- **Check:** `test_shader_cache.py` (`test_forked_workers_share_it`), `test_shader_toolchain_cache.py` (the batch loop with a substituted translator).

### A forked shader worker dies opening the compile cache

- **Symptom:** the Mac shader step stops a few seconds after it starts, every time, with `concurrent.futures.process.BrokenProcessPool` (since 8b55ebb3; the `[RED] a shader build worker process stopped` line once the build prints it), and `DMSH_PROCS=0` builds. The worker's crash report: `SIGSEGV` in `_os_log_preferences_refresh` ← `os_signpost_enabled` ← `os_signpost_id_make_with_pointer` ← `openDatabase` (`libsqlite3.dylib`) ← Python's `sqlite3`. Only with a Python whose `_sqlite3` module links Apple's `/usr/lib/libsqlite3.dylib` (`otool -L` on `_sqlite3.__file__`): Xcode's, and by the reports some Python 3.12+ builds; Homebrew's Python has its own SQLite and builds.
- **Cause:** Apple's SQLite calls `os_signpost` when it opens a database, and Apple's logging does not survive a fork without an exec once the process has used it: the child reads the parent's logging preferences at an address it does not have. The build opens `cas.db` at its start, and each forked worker opened a connection of its own (`Store.forked`). The same on macOS 27.0.1, 27.2 and 27.3, any game; `OS_ACTIVITY_MODE=disable` does not prevent it. A fork whose parent had opened no database opens one without harm, but the build needs the cache before its tables are complete, so the fork cannot come first.
- **Fix:** only the process that opened the cache uses it (`shader_cache.Served`). Each worker has a pipe to a thread of the build, which makes the worker's calls (`get`, `has`, `names`, `put`, `delete_prefix`, a batch) on the one connection under its lock, where the worker's thread stood when shaders were finished in threads; files are read and written by the worker (`put_file`, `to_file`). A worker's batch is its thread's transaction: whole or absent, and rolled back when the worker dies inside it. The workers stay forks, which is safe while they call nothing of Apple's frameworks themselves: their compilers and tools are exec'd.
- **Measurement:** Cuphead's 3,118 shaders on an M4 Pro (`J=14`, Homebrew's Python, an external drive), against main's workers with a connection each: an empty cache 49.5 and 44.9 s to 50.1, 53.3 and 47.4 s (61.8 s in threads), every shader restored from the cache 18.3 and 18.4 s to 18.8 and 16.7 s; the runs differ by more than the two designs do. `regress.py chain` on Cuphead: 3,131 of 3,131 shaders the same.
- **Open:** the reporters' Python builds are not confirmed (asked on the issue). Workers that are spawned, which would not depend on what a fork may call, were weighed and not built: each would read the build's tables again, which a fork shares. Up to the fork point the build takes 0.4 s and 58 MB on Cuphead and 3.7 s and 346 MB (peak resident) on Ocarina of Time's map, against about 35 MB for a forked worker, so 16 spawned workers there would hold up to about 5.5 GB instead of 0.6 GB; and `dmsh_build.py` would have to become a module whose start does not run a build (134 statements at its top level, the output clearing and the cache check among them).
- **Found on:** [DRAGON BALL: Sparking! ZERO](../../dm/games/010035F022078000/README.md#found-on-this-game) ([issue #2354](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2354); also Ocarina of Time on macOS 27.3, [issue #2362](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/2362)'s side note).
- **Check:** `test_shader_cache.py`: `test_forked_workers_with_apples_sqlite` runs the forked workers under `/usr/bin/python3` when its SQLite is Apple's (with a connection in each worker, all of them are killed), `test_worker_dying_in_a_batch`.

### A library that depends on its source's file name

- **Symptom:** two builds of the same shaders give different `_bl` or `_bf` libraries for some keys, of the same size, 46 bytes apart, while their MSL is the same. A byte comparison of two library folders reports them, and `regress.py` can report them as changed variants.
- **Cause:** clang names a source's static initializer after its file (`_GLOBAL__sub_I_<key>_bl.metal`), and any function constant brings one, so the name and the library's stored hash change with the file's base name (its folder does not matter). Sources without function constants do not depend on it. The MSL-keyed Metal cache takes the same text for the same bytes, so shaders that share a source took the library of whichever compiled first, or their own when they compiled at once. In Breath of the Wild 3,936 of 10,560 pixel shaders share their `_bl` source and 1,710 their `_bf` source.
- **Fix:** `dmsh_pch.compile`, which every compile of a shader library goes through, passes the source on standard input (`-x metal -`), so no file name reaches the compiler. Metal's messages then say `<stdin>`, and a failed compile also prints the source's path. Libraries from an earlier compile keep their bytes in the Metal cache: a cold build differs from them once, in `_bl` and `_bf`. The compile records and the chain version did not change, as the code the libraries hold did not. `_check` in `dmsh_mtlcache.py` samples only `_b`, and the offline pipeline archive leaves function constants to the device, so neither ever saw the difference.
- **Measurement:** cold builds at `J=4` and `J=14` of 471 Breath of the Wild shaders chosen from shared sources: 15 of 2,595 Mac files and 13 of 2,123 iOS files differed, all `_bl`; with the source on standard input none did. Pokopia's 1,001 at `J=6` and `J=14`: none of 4,581 Mac and 3,579 iOS files.
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Pokopia](../../dm/games/0400A3A01EE4C000/README.md#found-on-this-game) (resolved), [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md#found-on-this-game) (resolved).
- **Check:** `test_shader_pch.py` (`test_a_mac_library_does_not_depend_on_the_source_name`, `test_each_run_reads_the_source_from_its_start`).

### One compile for a pixel shader's side entry points

- **Symptom:** the Mac or iOS shader step of a game with many pixel shaders spends most of its Metal compiles on libraries that few draws load: Pokémon Pokopia's whole map took 366,805 compiles, about 57% of them for `_ba` and `_bl`, and its runs opened none of the 5,775 of each.
- **Cause:** the alpha test and the logic operations were each a whole copy of a pixel shader in a library of its own, compiled apart. Every pixel shader needs them, since which ones draw with either shows only in play: Xenoblade Chronicles 2, Skyward Sword HD, Dark Souls Remastered. Nothing in a game's files tells: every NVN game's binary names both functions, since the loader lists them all. A list per game would turn a miss into a wrong picture ([The alpha test through a library of its own](rendering.md#the-alpha-test-through-a-library-of-its-own), [Logic operations through a library of its own](rendering.md#logic-operations-through-a-library-of-its-own)), and compiling at run time has no `-O1`: GPU code grew by a median of 12% and up to 59%, Default and Size alike, and `-O2` took Odyssey's `alRenderMaterialUber` 25 minutes.
- **Fix:** `dm/gfx/tools/dmsh_side.py` makes one compile unit from a pixel shader's source: each entry point's body once, as a static function, and thin entry points around it, the shader's own and `<entry>_a` (`dmsh_alpha.py`) and `<entry>_l` (`dmsh_logic.py`). `metallib` gives every entry point a complete module whether or not the body is shared, and `metal-tt` compiles every unspecialized entry point of a library, which made the pipeline archive twice as large (1.85 to 3.84 MB for 25 bases). So `cut` splits the library in two, `<key>_b` with the shader's own entry points (+0.6% of archive) and `<key>_bx` with the others, loaded on first need (`at_fn`, `lo_fn`). A cut of one function is byte-identical to `metallib --function-name`, which takes only one name. The Metal cache keeps both pieces under the unit's digest (`compile_split`), so outputs stay clones. `<key>_b.metal` stays the plain source, and `dmsh_mtlcache.py` fill leaves out a library with a `_bx`, which was not compiled from it. A shader whose unit does not build gets its base from the plain source, no side library and a red line, and the host draws it without them. The readable-tile entry is a third piece of that same compile ([Cold Mac shader builds compile each pixel shader more than once](#cold-mac-shader-builds-compile-each-pixel-shader-more-than-once)).
- **Cost:** a shared body changes the inliner's decisions (a helper now has two callers), so `main0` is no longer byte-identical: GPU code +2.1% at the median and +3.7% at most, on 125 shaders of six games on an M4 Pro, pipeline builds 62.5 against 60.6 ms. Keeping `<key>_b` as it was with a second compile for `<key>_bx` would cost 66-68% of the three compiles' CPU instead of 34-36%.
- **Measurement:** Metal CPU per pixel shader on 125 shaders of six games, three compiles to one: Pokopia 1.503 to 0.512 s with the Mac's flags and 0.830 to 0.285 s with the iPhone's; the others 0.324 to 0.114 s and 0.245 to 0.087 s. Cold builds of 1,021 Pokopia shaders (`J=10`, other jobs on the Mac): Metal compiles 2,444 to 1,095 on the Mac and 2,445 to 1,094 for iOS; the whole build's CPU 1,399 to 670 s and 959 to 451 s; wall 273 to 195 s and 232 to 127 s; libraries 359 to 358 MB. All 1,021 `_b.metal` came out unchanged, and their reflections but for the chain version and the entry point names. Not measured on a device.
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Pokopia](../../dm/games/0400A3A01EE4C000/README.md#found-on-this-game).
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_side.py`, `test_dmsh_alpha.py`, `test_dmsh_logic.py`, `make check_t_alpha`, `make check_t_logic`.

### Screen UVs a pixel shader computes from the clip position

- **Symptom:** Xenoblade Chronicles in battle on an iPhone drops to single-digit frame rates; the log's `same-pass feedback sampling: snapshot #…` count climbs by thousands a frame and no draw reads the tile. S2Arm measured 2,000 to 3,600 whole-attachment snapshots a frame (about 3 GB) and no tile reads; in Xenoblade Chronicles 3 on a Mac, every snapshot that could have been a tile read was refused for lack of a readable-tile version.
- **Cause:** the engine's G-buffer decals and lights sample the targets of their own pass at their pixel, but write that position another way than [the decals the tile read was made for](rendering.md#same-pass-feedback-read-from-the-tile): the vertex shader passes the clip position itself as an output, and the pixel shader turns it into the screen UV, `fma(x / w, 0.5, 0.5)` and `fma(y / w, -0.5, 0.5)`. `dmsh_fetch.py` knew only `x / w`, `y / w` of the clip → screen varying, so no readable-tile version (`<key>_bf`) was made. Most of these shaders also write 5 or 6 targets, past the readable-tile version's limit of 4 outputs.
- **Fix:** for every game, in the chain and the host. `dmsh_fetch.fold` takes a sample at `float2(fma(P.x, 0.5, 0.5), fma(P.y, -0.5, 0.5))`, P a projective component pair of one output, and marks it `proj` and `ndc`; `screen_positions` proves on a vertex shader's source that an output is `gl_Position` itself (.x, .y, .w) in every entry point, and the reflection lists those outputs as `screen_pos` (only when there are any). The host (`nvn_refl.h` `fc_ndc`, `scr_pos`; `nvn_mtl.m` `scr_proof`) proves an NDC sample with `scr_pos` where a projective one takes `screen`, with the other conditions unchanged (the viewport over the whole target, an upper-left origin, no geometry or tessellation stage). The readable-tile version takes up to 8 outputs (`OM_j_k` = 64 + 8 j + k, 64 to 127: NX 18 has no other function constant below 200, so S2Arm's move of outputs 5 to 8 to 128 is not needed); past 4 outputs only with a sample the host can prove (projective, NDC or pixel position) or a `bf_want.tsv` row, as a version with only samples at a plain interpolated output is never drawn. NX 18's `main0_f` calls `main0`'s shared body and declares its own output struct, so S2Arm's other two changes, a sample mask member it adds itself and a `return` inside a one-line block, do not arise; an output struct with a game's own `[[sample_mask]]` gets no readable-tile version, as before. A chain change. Ported from S2Arm `4804ac50` and `69f75818` (Apache-2.0), rewritten against NX 18's code.
- **Check:** `python3 dm/gfx/tests/test_dmsh_fetch.py` (`NdcSample`, `ScreenPositions`, `EightOutputs`: both platforms compile the 8-output version); `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_feedback` (`ndc_case`: an NDC draw reads the tile on the GPU after a barrier, and takes a snapshot without `scr_pos`), `check_t_refl` (`ndc`, `screen_pos`, 8 outputs, a ninth refused), `check_t_queue_origin` (the NDC proof follows the viewport origin). A read of Xenoblade Chronicles 3's current library (chain `ae1dd68dc11c`) through the new `fold`: 2,213 more readable-tile versions, each with an NDC sample (2,104 of them with more than 4 outputs), 465 existing ones with an NDC sample added, and 7,663 vertex shaders with a `screen_pos` output.
- **Found on:** [Xenoblade Chronicles 3](../../dm/games/010074F013262000/README.md) (S2Arm's `038af36eb178a2bf` with 6 outputs and `a93aa7a906dc4e2d` with 5) and [Xenoblade Chronicles: Definitive Edition](../../dm/games/0100FF500E34A000/README.md), the same engine (1,277 more readable-tile versions read the same way). Not yet measured here: the frame rate and snapshot count in battle on the iPhone.

### Cold Mac shader builds compile each pixel shader more than once

- **Symptom:** the first Mac shader build of a title, with an empty output directory and CAS and no keys, takes tens of minutes while `J` Metal workers stay busy. On an Apple M2 MacBook Pro at `J=8`, Mario Kart 8 Deluxe took 22:46 (1366 s) and Super Mario Odyssey 30:02 (1802 s).
- **Cause:** a pixel shader's code went through the Metal compiler several times. The readable-tile version `<key>_bf` was a source and a compile of its own (`fetch_finish`): 13,666 of Mario Kart 8 Deluxe's 29,651 shaders and 10,512 of Odyssey's 27,984. The integer entry points `main0_u` / `main0_m` (`fs_uint`) and the dual-source `main0_d` (`fs_dual`) were each a copy of `main0`'s body, which the compiler built again for each: 4,990 of Pokémon Legends: Arceus's 6,160 pixel shaders have `main0_u`. And each compile launched `metal` and `metallib` through `xcrun`, which looks the tool up again every time (about 4 ms a launch).
- **Fix:** for every game. `dmsh_side.shared_body` makes `main0`'s body one `always_inline` function, `dm_body_main0`, which `main0`, `main0_u`, `main0_m` and `main0_d` call. `dmsh_fetch.fold` rewrites the rewritable samples in that body alone, as `(fx_i == k ? fb_k : sample)` with `fx_i` and `fb0`–`fb7` parameters of the body, and adds `main0_f`, which passes its function constants and the tile's colours; every other caller passes -1 and zeros, which the inlined body folds back to the plain sample, so `main0` and the side entry points declare no function constants. `ps_libraries` cuts `<key>_b`, `<key>_bx` and `<key>_bf` from the one compile (`dmsh_side.cut` with groups, `dmsh_mtlcache.compile_split` with a list); the host's `bf_fn` loads `main0_f`. What does not build is left out alone: a unit whose readable-tile version does not build is compiled again without it, one that does not build at all gives the base from the plain source, each with a red line. A shader with no rewritable sample gets `main0_f` only when its key is in `bf_want.tsv`, as before. `dmsh_build.py` finds `metal` and `metallib` once (`xcrun -f`) and passes `-isysroot` with the SDK, which gives the same `.air` and `.metallib` bytes as `xcrun -sdk macosx`; `MAC_RECORD` still names the `xcrun` command. Two consequences: a library cut with a `_bf` is not taken by `dmsh_mtlcache.py` fill (its base was not compiled from its `.metal`), and `dmsh_side.py` is part of the shader cache key (`CODE_FP`), since `fs_uint` and `fs_dual` call it when a shader misses the cache.
- **Measurement:** cold builds, `own_compilers`, `DMSH_TARGETS=mac`, no keys, `J=8`, Apple M2 MacBook Pro, of PR #1520's first form (the same fold, with copies still in `main0_d`): Mario Kart 8 Deluxe 22:46 (1366 s) to 14:47 (887 s), 1.54×; Super Mario Odyssey 30:02 (1802 s) to 22:23 (1343 s), 1.34×; every shader built in both. Metal CPU on 60 of Odyssey's pixel shaders (Apple M6): 25.8 to 13.6 s for 40 with a readable-tile version (0.53×), 30.5 to 19.6 s for 20 with integer entry points only (0.64×; `007d7e411ef14ebd` 24.5 to 13.6 s); the fold applied to the same 60 shaders as before; `main0`'s bitcode 5.7% smaller at the median and 0.7% larger at most. The `xcrun` lookup was about 4 of a launch's 10 ms with 2 GB resident; launching with `posix_spawn` instead of `subprocess` saved another 0.4–1.2 ms, which is not worth code of its own. Partial cold builds of the same pixel shaders on the previous chain and this one (Apple M6, translation included): Pokémon Legends: Arceus 239 in 24 to 18 s, Metroid Prime Remastered 182 in 26 to 19 s, each shader with the same libraries, entry points, side and readable-tile records and reflection, none failing (MPR's `ab809a7456f59614` has both `main0_d` and `main0_f`). GPU code and pipeline build time not measured.
- **Found on:** [Mario Kart 8 Deluxe](../../dm/games/0100152000022000/README.md#found-on-this-game), [Super Mario Odyssey](../../dm/games/0100000000010000/README.md#found-on-this-game).
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_fetch.py` (`OtherEntryPoints`: a `main0_d`, a copy of the body and a compile unit beside `main0_f`; `OutputMappingOnly`), `test_dmsh_side.py`, `test_shader_toolchain_cache.py`, `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_feedback check_t_alpha check_t_logic`.

### One compile for a vertex shader's libraries

- **Symptom:** a cold Mac or iOS shader build spends most of its Metal compiles on vertex shaders' copies of one body. Breath of the Wild's 19,592 shaders took 21,552 compiles, 2,405 of the build's 3,692 CPU seconds (240 s of wall on an M5 Pro at `J=18`): 839 s of that went to pulling libraries and 109 s to shadow-input libraries, each a second and third compile of a body `<key>_b` already holds three times.
- **Cause:** a vertex shader's body went through the optimizer nine times in two compiles, twelve in three for a shader with integer inputs. `<key>_b` held it in `main0`, `main0_z` (`vs_depth`) and `main0_gs` (`dmsh_geometry.capture`); the pulling library `<key>_bp` (`dmsh_pull.py`), a source and a compile of its own, in `main0`, `main0_z` and twice in each wide-line entry; the shadow-input library `<key>_bv` (`dmsh_vin.py`) in three more. A pixel shader's entry points had the same shape before [One compile for a pixel shader's side entry points](#one-compile-for-a-pixel-shaders-side-entry-points).
- **Fix:** for every game. `dmsh_side.vs_unit` makes one compile unit of a vertex shader that has a pulling library: the body once, as `dm_vs_body`, `always_inline` in the entry points pipelines are made of (`main0`, `main0_z`, the pulling `main0_p` / `main0_zp`, the shadow-input `main0_v` / `main0_zv`), and one out-of-line copy, `dm_vs_body_o`, for the wide-line entries `main0_wl` / `main0_z_wl`, which few draws take and which ran the body twice each. `vs_libraries` in `dmsh_build.py` compiles the unit once a target and cuts `<key>_b`, `<key>_bp` and `<key>_bv` from it (`dmsh_side.cut`, `dmsh_mtlcache.compile_split`), so each library holds the entry points it held and `<key>_b.metal` stays the plain source. One unit cannot name its entry points all `main0`: the reflection's `pull` and `vin` name them, and the host (`side_entry` in `nvn_mtl.m`) and `pso_offline.py` (`vertex_entry`) read the names. What stays as it was:
  - `main0_gs` and the rarer entry points (`main0_discard`, `main0_tf`, the layer ones) are whole copies of the body.
  - A shader without a pulling library (no stage input, or one `dmsh_pull.variant` does not fit) compiles its plain source.
  - A shader with layer entry points keeps `<key>_bv` a compile of its own: that library repeats `main0_l` for the host (`vin_fn`), and one unit cannot hold it twice (8 of Pokémon Scarlet's shaders, none of the two Zelda games').
  - With `DMSH_IOS_PULL=0` the iPhone's unit leaves the pulling entry points out, and with no shadow-input library either its `<key>_b` is the plain source's compile.
  - A unit that does not build, or a source of a shape `vs_unit` does not know, gets the three compiles with a red line (`its libraries do not build from one compile`). None of the 38,402 shaders tested used this fallback. `dmsh_mtlcache.py` fills the standalone cache from earlier builds. Each target's reflection records `chain_metal_cut` when its base came from a unit, so the cache skips that base even after iOS packing removes its pulling library. A synthetic test packs that image with two standalone shaders that pass the cache's sample check and verifies that a later standalone compile produces its own library.
  - NX 18 (ported from upstream b989137a5, with the wide-line entries of upstream 7eaf79116): units carry `main0_wl` / `main0_z_wl` and their out-of-line copy `dm_vs_body_o` as upstream's do, and `test_dmsh_vsunit.py`, `t_vs_unit.m` and `t_pso_bundle.m` check upstream's shape; only the compile flags come from NX 18's own places (`ios_metal.METAL`, `dmsh_build.py` `MAC_METAL`). NX 18's `t_vs_unit` passes (86 checks on an M-series Mac, macOS 27).
- **Forms measured and not taken:**
  - The body as a plain `static` function that every entry point calls is one optimization of the body for all of them (64% less Metal CPU a vertex shader, against 41%), but the GPU compiler then decides the inlining for each pipeline: `main0`'s bitcode grew 4% at the median and pipeline builds took 1.3 to 3.7% longer. Not without a measurement on a device.
  - The out-of-line copy is never `noinline`. Marked so and shared with `main0_gs`, it changed that entry's captured words in 24 of 52 shaders, by up to 12,544 units in the last place.
  - `main0_gs` calling the body, with its capture epilogue once after the call instead of at every return, saves more (53% instead of 41%) and captured other low bits in 1 of 52 shaders (Tears of the Kingdom's `0ef972796032c959`, six returns: 186 words of 18 of 6,000 vertices, by up to 1,778 units in the last place). `main0_gs` returns nothing and is not invariant; it is left for a change of its own.
- **Cost:** every output compared on the GPU is bit for bit what the three compiles gave (Measurement). The libraries are not byte-identical. Entry point by entry point, over the 38,402 shaders of three games: `main0_gs`, `main0_discard`, `main0_tf` and the layer entry points are the same bitcode in every shader; `main0_z` in 36,250; `main0` in 7,628, since it applies the depth line once, after the call, where the copy had it before every return (0.3 to 0.4% smaller at the median, between 2.4% smaller and 0.8% larger); the pulling and shadow-input entry points have other names, within 1.2% smaller and 0.5% larger; the wide-line entries, which call the out-of-line copy, between 3.4% smaller and 7.3% larger (0.1 to 0.3% smaller at the median). `<key>_bv` is half its size (Breath of the Wild's 50.1 to 27.0 MB): it no longer repeats `main0_gs`.
- **Measurement:** on an M5 Pro, macOS 26.7.3, GPU `applegpu_g17s`.
  - Metal CPU for a vertex shader's libraries (compile and link, the best of three, 40 shaders evenly spread a game): Breath of the Wild 216.3 to 127.0 ms with the Mac's flags (41.3% less) and 218.4 to 128.1 ms with the iPhone's; Tears of the Kingdom 225.7 to 135.3 ms (40.1% less) and 225.3 to 135.5 ms.
  - A cold Mac build of Breath of the Wild (`own_compilers.py`, `DMSH_TARGETS=mac`, `J=18`, an idle Mac), the commit before against this one: wall 240.4 to 188.8 s, CPU 3,692 to 2,859 s, Metal compiles 21,552 to 13,957 (2,405 to 1,685 s), links 21,550 to 13,955 (413 to 311 s). All 8,888 shaders with a pulling library came from a unit.
  - `regress.py chain` on Breath of the Wild, Tears of the Kingdom and Pokémon Scarlet: every shader builds on both sides (19,592, 28,669 and 30,142), no MSL changes, and the reflections and side libraries of 8,888, 14,549 and 14,965 vertex shaders change (the names); no pipeline base's interface fails.
  - On the GPU, 26 vertex shaders of each Zelda game (16 evenly spread, the 4 largest sources, 6 more with integer inputs), 2,000 random triangles each (6,000 vertices; random but valid buffers, uniforms and textures, not the games' own data), 512 × 512: the depth image and every `float4` output of `main0`, `main0_z`, `main0_p` and `main0_zp` (26 of 26 shaders a game) and of `main0_v` and `main0_zv` (13 of 13 and 9 of 9) are bit-identical to those of the plain source's `main0` / `main0_z`; so are `main0_wl`'s images (26 of 26) and `main0_gs`'s 22,464,000 captured words a game. Controls: `x` one unit in the last place larger is seen in 49 of the 52 shaders, the depth line's 0.5 one unit larger in all 52.
  - Open measurements include iPhone GPU comparisons, device pipeline build times and production archive sizes.
- **Found on:** [Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md#found-on-this-game), [Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md#found-on-this-game).
- **Check:** `test_dmsh_vsunit.py` (the unit's text, its cut libraries on both compilers, `main0_gs` byte for byte the plain source's, the build step with its fallbacks), `test_shader_toolchain_cache.py` (the Metal cache's fill), `test_pso_offline.py`; `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_vs_unit` (every entry point of a unit against the three compiles on the GPU, with a control), `check_t_pso_bundle` (the reflection's names in the host and the offline archive), `check_t_pull_inv check_t_vin`.

### Shader-only prewarm lines loaded and recorded that build no pipeline

- **Symptom:** none on screen: memory, background CPU and power in the first minutes, and lists that only grow. Every owner game's lists hold thousands of `S` lines (a shader alone, no pipeline): Ocarina of Time 19,607 of 33,410 rows, Tears of the Kingdom 16,813, Breath of the Wild 12,874, Donkey Kong Bananza 8,159, Xenoblade Chronicles 3 7,990, Mario Kart World 6,552. Those no `R`, `M` or `C` line uses load after the first frame on two utility workers (`pipeline prewarm after the first frame: …`) and stay in the shader cache for the run, and each start records more (`pso_user.tsv`).
- **Cause:** a pipeline line already loads its own shaders in the prewarm, a program's creation prefetches its libraries (`mtl_prog_prefetch`) and a draw loads what it binds, so an `S` line alone builds nothing. They were recorded at every library load (`shader_get`, the look-ahead's `shader_prefetch_rest`, the claim of a prefetched library in `warm_claim`), and the prewarm loaded them all. The prepare step's split ([`DM_PSO_LATE_S`](../development.md#the-prepare-step)) moved them after the first frame but still loaded them.
- **Fix:** general, `nvn_mtl.m` `drop_s_on` (`DM_PSO_DROP_S`, on by default; `=0` records, loads and filters `S` rows exactly as before). Adapted from upstream NXNative `28d41fc9d` (seedds). Nothing records an `S` row (`pso_user_put` also drops one recorded anyway); `warm_parse_h` ignores them in the lists, before its repeat set, and counts them (the `pipeline prewarm done: …` line ends with `; shader-only (S) lines ignored N`; its `shaders` count, and the `pipeline prepare: … lines (N shaders, …)` line's, are then the libraries the pipeline lines load, each once, in `warm_shader`, where they counted only `S` tasks and read 0), so `warm_split_late` has none left and nothing loads after the first frame; the device list's filter leaves them out of its set (`DM_PSO_USER_HASH`'s `pso_hset_add_list`, and the `=0` string set), since none is ever looked up. A Mac recording (`DM_PSO_RECORD`) now gets the `R` row of a draw a hybrid stand-in served too (`demand_record`), as `pso_user.tsv` did: its shaders had only their `S` rows there before. NX 18 keeps its utility QoS for the prefetch (upstream moved `mtl_prog_prefetch` to user-initiated queues; not taken) and its late-`S` path for `=0`. The committed lists keep their `S` rows in this change, so `=0` still loads them: `dm/tools/pso_seed.py strip-s --game <id>` takes them out of a list when wanted, and refuses a cut that would change the shader-build digest (`dmsh_chain.py --inputs`: only `G` and `K` rows) or the rows the offline archive reads (`pso_offline.py`: `C`, `M`, `R`). Checked over every list in the tree: 96 lists, 284,548 `S` rows, no digest and no archive row changed. Stripping still changes a list's bytes, so the NSP script copies the profile again (its split and shaders are kept) and the first start after it waits for the prepare step once (`prepared.tsv`'s stamp hashes the lists). `pso_seed.py add` skips a recorded list's `S` rows by default, unchecked, and prints how many (`--keep-s` or `DM_PSO_DROP_S=0` adds them as before).
- **Measured (Mac, M4 Max, a harness over `warm_parse` and `pso_hset_add_list` on the committed lists, best of 5; `=0` → default):** Ocarina of Time: 7,996 libraries no longer loaded after the first frame (the `S` lines no pipeline line uses), the parsed task list 11.7 → 9.5 MB, the parse 173 → 154 ms, the filter set 1.0 → 0.25 MB. Tears of the Kingdom: 6,063 libraries, 14.4 → 12.4 MB, 242 → 224 ms, 1.0 → 0.5 MB. Breath of the Wild: 5,105 libraries. Donkey Kong Bananza: 3,293 libraries, 7.2 → 6.3 MB, parse unchanged (250 ms: its `R` rows dominate). Xenoblade Chronicles 3: 6,007 libraries. Mario Kart World: 366. Persona 5 Royal: 30,357 `S` of 32,316 rows, parse 66 → 37 ms, 5.2 → 1.6 MB. What a library left unloaded saves on the phone (its read, its reflection and its resident memory, on utility workers in the first minutes of play) is not measured.
- **Risk:** a shader only an `S` line named, which no program creation prefetches before its first draw, loads at that draw (a library load, not a pipeline compile). The prefetch at program creation covers the usual case.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_warm` (`drop_s`: the `S` lines are no lines and no tasks and are counted, the late split gets none; a draw's own load, a prefetched library's claim and a direct record write no `S` row to either list; a stand-in's `R` row reaches both; the filter's fill leaves `S` rows out; each again under `=0`, which writes and loads them as before; with the change undone, 7 of these checks fail), `check_t_pso_user_hash` (the hash and string filters against a reference that drops `S` rows, and under `DM_PSO_DROP_S=0` against the old one), `check_t_pso_later` (both settings), `check_t_pso_user_queue`; `python3 -m unittest dm/tools/tests/test_pso_seed.py` (`NoSRows`: add skips, `--keep-s` and `=0` keep, strip-s on a copy of Super Mario RPG's profile with the same digest, and refused when a digest that reads the list is put in).
- **Phone check:** a start of a game with many `S` lines (Ocarina of Time or Tears of the Kingdom): the log has `pipeline prewarm done: … ; shader-only (S) lines ignored N` with N in the thousands and no `pipeline prewarm after the first frame` line; the memory line after a minute of play is lower than with `DM_PSO_DROP_S=0`; no new `S` rows in `Documents/pso_user.tsv`; first visits to new areas show no new hitches (`DM_PSO_WAITLOG=1` lists pipeline waits; a library-load hitch would show as a slow frame at a first draw).

### A later start waited for prewarm lists it had prepared before

- **Symptom:** every start, not only the first, holds the first frame for the prepare step: `pipeline prepare: N lines … in … s … before the first frame` with nearly every base `from the archive` and none compiled. A large list costs seconds at each start (on the iPhone one lookup in Ocarina of Time's bundled archive takes about 50 ms).
- **Cause:** the step ran every line of the lists before the first frame at every start, on purpose: a stamp that skipped it would skip it exactly when the system had dropped the Metal cache. An archive hit is still a lookup and a library load per line.
- **Fix:** the step is not skipped but moved: a start whose lists this device prepared to the end before, in this archive, hands every line to the background on half the performance cores at utility QoS and the first frame goes on (`warm_later_ready`, `warm_later_bg`, `warm_later_mark` in `dm/gfx/nvn/nvn_mtl.m`; [the prepare step](../development.md#the-prepare-step) has the stamp, `pso_archive/prepared.tsv`, and what makes a start wait). Idea from S2Arm `e899d98a1`, NX 18's own code. `DM_PSO_LATER_START=0` waits as before.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_later`: a first start waits and writes the stamp, the next hands its list on while its first library load is held at the device, takes every base from the archive and claims the pipeline at the next submission; a changed list, `DM_PSO_LATER_START=0` and a refused archive file each wait.
- **Open:** not measured on a device: the first frame's time on a second start (the start line's `prepare` part, and `pipeline prepare in the background: …` for how long the lines take behind it), and whether the first seconds of play wait on a pipeline the background has not reached (`DM_PSO_WAITLOG=1`).

### Concurrent warm tasks prepare each shader once

- **Symptom:** startup tasks can prepare the same shader more than once, even though only one object enters the warm dictionary. A measured Tears of the Kingdom startup avoided 320 duplicate preparations; this is not a steady-frame-rate claim.
- **Cause:** an `S` task and an `R` task, or two shader pairs, can miss the same key before either publishes. `shader_load` and the pulling-entry preparation used to run independently for each miss. Discarding the losing `NvShader` does not undo the strong function-descriptor registrations (`fn4_note`, `fn_spec`) or constant-bank reservations (`c1_store`). These ownership mechanisms are deliberate; source inspection alone does not establish a leak or its size.
- **Fix:** `warm_shader` installs a per-key `WarmLoad` under the dictionary lock. The owner prepares the library, reflection, functions, argument encoder, c1 storage and optional pulling entry outside that lock, then publishes before releasing its waiters. A cached lookup allocates nothing. Other keys can prepare concurrently. A failed warm load stays `NSNull` only for that prewarm, matching the previous warm-call behavior; `warm_finish` drops warm dictionaries after all tasks join, and demand loading retains its existing retry behavior. This does not coalesce demand/prefetch loads racing a background prewarm (`DM_PSO_PREPARE=0`), nor merge layer variants. The other loading paths share preparations since "Every shader loading path prepares each shader once" below.
- **Measurement:** a Tears of the Kingdom startup probe observed 1,537 distinct shader keys. The old racing path performed 1,857 preparations and discarded 320 successful duplicates; per-key coalescing performed 1,537 preparations and discarded none. Observed startup times were 6.63 s before and 6.51 s / 7.15 s with coalescing, so these runs establish duplicate avoidance, not a wall-time improvement or a memory leak. A Breath of the Wild 1.9.0 probe on the same Mac and chain went from 844 preparations of 819 keys to 819, at 3.07 s before and 2.78 s / 2.83 s with coalescing: the same limit applies.
- **Found on:** [Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md#found-on-this-game), through source review of the shared prewarm lookup/load/publish path and a normal-start Mac probe; [Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md#found-on-this-game), the same probe.
- **Regression seam:** `t_pso_warm` parses actual `S` and `R` manifest tasks sharing a vertex key and gates the first real `MTLDevice` library load. A test-owned dictionary proxy observes the second same-key lookup under the production dictionary lock before the owner is released; a third key must finish independently while the owner is held. Actual device library-load counts check that the shared key is prepared once and cached hits do not reload it; both callers retain one shader identity. It also checks warm negative caching and a later successful demand retry after reflection appears. The existing `check_t_pso_warm` target runs both MTLDevice and Metal 4 paths.

### Every shader loading path prepares each shader once

- **Symptom:** the same shader library is prepared twice when two loading paths miss it close together: a draw loads a library its program's prefetch is still loading, the look-ahead loads one a prefetch or a shader-only line after the first frame has in flight, or the prewarm loads one the game's own paths already loaded. Each copy reads the library and reflection again and keeps its own function-descriptor and constant-bank registrations; the extra copy is dropped at the claim. No frame-rate, memory or battery gain is measured or claimed.
- **Cause:** "Concurrent warm tasks prepare each shader once" coalesced only the prewarm's own tasks. The draw's own load (`shader_get`), the program-creation prefetch (`mtl_prog_prefetch`), the look-ahead's parallel load (`shader_prefetch_rest`) and the shader-only lines after the first frame (`warm_late_one`) each called `shader_load` (and `pull_load`) on their own; `progpf_first` kept only the prefetch and the late lines from queueing a name twice.
- **Fix:** all five paths load through `shader_acquire`: one record per library name in `g_shload` (stage and depth entry with it). The first caller owns the load and holds the record's latch, an unfair lock, across it, so a draw that joins a utility-QoS prefetch lends it its priority; the table lock is never held across a load or a wait. Others join the record and get the same `NvShader`; a caller after it finishes takes the finished shader. A queued prefetch block has no record until it runs, so a draw never waits for the queue. A failed preparation hands nil to those that joined it and leaves the table, so the next caller tries again. Finished records stay for the process, as `g_shcache`'s entries do (the same objects); `warm_shader` keeps its per-prewarm negative cache. A joiner that wants the pulling entry takes it through `pull_fn` (locked), since the shader may already be in use. Layer variants (`shader_get_layer`) are not shared. Adapted from ComicBit/nxnative bee12ec (MIT), whose own commit claims no FPS, thermal or retained-byte gain; its queued-ticket stealing and per-shader pull lock are left out. `DM_NVN_SHADER_SHARE=0` turns it off: each path prepares its own copy again, as before.
- **Measurement:** not measured on a game yet. The log shows what it saves: `shader sharing: N preparations joined, M made` at each power of two of joined preparations, and `shader sharing so far: …` at the prewarm's end; an A/B with `DM_NVN_SHADER_SHARE=0` compares startup and first-visit loads.
- **Found on:** ComicBit's BOTW capture (695 repeated preparations across producers, mostly sequential), through source review of NX 18's loading paths.
- **Regression seam:** `t_pso_warm` `shared_paths` holds the prewarm's real library load at the device while a draw's load (`shader_get`) and a late shader-only line (`warm_late_one`) ask for the same key: one library load, one made and two joined, the same shader for all three. A missing library fails, leaves the table, and the next caller loads it once it exists. `g_shshare = 0` loads two copies and records nothing. Weak references check that the shader and its record are released with the caches.

### A prebuild pool sized from the Super cores

- **Symptom:** the Mac's pipeline prebuild (`DM_PSO_PREBUILD=tool`, the NSP script's `pso_prebuild.log`) runs on far fewer workers than the Mac has: `1 workers` on an M6, where Metroid Prime Remastered's 20,233 pairs took 1,725 s and Link's Awakening's 17,742 took 667 s, and 4 on an M5 Pro, where Donkey Kong Bananza's 21,225 took 6.5 to 7.5 minutes.
- **Cause:** each worker waits on one synchronous compile, and the tool's pool was the "performance cores" less one, read from `hw.perflevel0.physicalcpu`. On the chips with three core levels, level 0 is only the Super cores: 2 on an M6 (beside 4 Performance and 6 Efficiency), 5 on an M5 Pro (beside 10 others). Metal runs 6 and 14 compiles at once there (`maximumConcurrentCompilationTaskCount`).
- **Fix:** the tool has `maximumConcurrentCompilationTaskCount` workers (`compile_tasks`; the active cores where Metal gives less than 2). `DM_PSO_PREBUILD_THREADS` still sets them. The prepare step keeps the active cores: extra workers only wait, and no iPhone has reported its count. **Open:** one rule for both pools needs an iPhone's count and first-start time. The queues of the builds hybrid draws ask for (`pp_bg_queue`) are now one per two level-0 cores but at least half of Metal's count, up to 3 (`pp_bg_count`; `DM_PSO_BG_QUEUES=<n>` sets them): level 0 alone gave 1 queue on an M6 and on an iPhone 18 Pro Max, where a backlog of 323 builds in Super Mario Odyssey drained one at a time over about 20 s. The log's `pipeline builds in the background` line gives the count and Metal's; no iPhone run has reported them yet, so the iPhone's count and the backlog's time with it are open. The prebuild without a prepare step still takes a share of level 0.
- **Measurement:** M5 Pro, macOS 27.0 (26A428), the prebuild tool on Bananza's app libraries (chain `2612ffd16287`) with hosts built from that app's commit without and with the change, other jobs' Metal compiles running beside both. All 21,225 pairs: 393 and 452 s on 4 workers, 187 and 272 s on 14 (1.8 times faster on average). M6, macOS 27.2, Skyward Sword's 2,125 pairs from an empty archive, another session's shader build beside them, in the quietest of three rounds: 45.1 s on 1 worker, 28.0 on 3, 19.2 on 6 (Metal's count), 21.3 on 9, 18.8 on 12. The host with this fix, Metroid Prime Remastered's first 2,000 pairs on the M6: 52 s on its default 6 workers from a cold cache, 194 s on 1 worker afterwards. **`shouldMaximizeConcurrentCompilation` is not set:** Metal reports the same count with it and without it on both Macs, its effect did not rise above the noise on either (M5 Pro, from a cold cache: 62 pairs/s on 4 workers without it, 53 with it, 98 on 14 without it, 112 with it), and [Apple's guide](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/metal/using-the-metal-4-compilation-api.md) gives it as the Metal 3 setting, while the prebuild compiles through `MTL4Compiler`.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md#found-on-this-game).
- **Check:** `t_pso_warm` checks that the tool's default follows Metal's count.

### Function constants specialized at load

- **Symptom:** `fragmentFunction main0 cannot be used to build a pipeline state` (a Metal validation assertion).
- **Cause:** SPIRV-Cross declares an optional function constant, `spvLinearTextureAlignmentOverride`, in shaders that read a texel buffer, and Metal refuses any function with function constants until it is specialized.
- **Fix:** `shader_load` specializes such functions with an empty set of values. On the Metal 4 path the pipeline's descriptor specializes them, not the library: [Functions with function constants specialized by the pipeline, not the library](#functions-with-function-constants-specialized-by-the-pipeline-not-the-library).
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Legends: Z-A](../../dm/games/0100F43008C44000/README.md) (one imgui pixel shader).

### Functions with function constants specialized by the pipeline, not the library

- **Symptom:** with Metal 4 pipelines on (then the default; now the only path), Apple's performance HUD says `Compiled 2 shaders. Total compilation time is 102.70ms. Consider adopting MTL4Compiler for more fine grained compilations` during play, when a shader with function constants loads.
- **Cause:** a function that declares function constants must be specialized before it goes into a pipeline ([Function constants specialized at load](#function-constants-specialized-at-load)). The host did that through `MTLLibrary`'s `newFunctionWithName:constantValues:error:` when it loaded the shader (`shader_load`) and the libraries made from it (the pulling and shadow-input vertex entries, `pull_load` and `vin_fn`; the alpha test, `at_fn`). That call compiles through the MTLDevice, synchronously, on the thread that loads the shader. A Metal 4 pipeline does not need it: `fn4_note` already gives MTL4Compiler an `MTL4SpecializedFunctionDescriptor` with the same values.
- **Fix:** `fn_spec` in `nvn_mtl.m`. The `MTLFunction` stays unspecialized and is noted with its constant values, so MTL4Compiler specializes it with the pipeline. Its reflection and argument encoders are the same as the specialized function's. At first a pipeline the `MTLDevice` built took a function specialized through the library once, under a stable record per function that gave overlapping first uses one shared result. Since the Metal 4-only release there is no `MTLDevice` pipeline, so nothing specializes through the library: at first compute shaders were still specialized at load for the `MTLDevice`'s compute pipelines, a pipeline whose function had no Metal 4 descriptor (then the `DM_NVN_HILITE` override) took the `MTLDevice` with a function `fn_dev` specialized through the library, and the variants that differ only in their constant values (the logic operation entries, `lo_fn`; the readable-tile versions, `bf_fn`) specialized through the library because `newFunctionWithName:` hands back the same object for one name. Now compute shaders are noted with their values as any other stage, the diagnostic overrides have Metal 4 descriptors, and `lo_fn` and `bf_fn` go through `fn_spec` too, with a stand-in object (`FnSpec`) per set of values so that each set is noted on an object of its own.
- **Found on:** [Animal Crossing: New Horizons](../../dm/games/01006F8002326000/README.md#found-on-this-game) on an iPhone 17 Pro Max with Metal 4 pipelines turned on: the HUD warning when a shader loaded during play. On the Mac, with a probe that logs each MTLDevice compile, the specializations came from `shader_load` during the prepare step and again when a shader loaded during play. After the fix none were left, and the picture was the same.
- **Check:** `t_pso_archive`. A function with a constant stays unspecialized, its descriptor specializes it, and a pipeline is made through the Metal 4 compiler; `fn_spec` asks the library for no specialization (a counting library sees none). A set of constants of its own is an object and a note of its own, and its note specializes it. A function with no descriptor gives no pipeline.

### Layer-writing vertex shaders get the triangle class

- **Symptom:** a pipeline for a vertex shader that writes `render_target_array_index` cannot be built.
- **Cause:** Metal builds such a pipeline only with an input primitive class, and the translator writes a point size in every vertex shader, which needs the point class: no class fits both.
- **Fix:** `vs_layer` drops the point size from vertex shaders that write the layer, and the host gives their pipelines the triangle class (`pso_desc`) by the reflection's `layer_out` ([Vertex shaders with layer entry points left out of the prebuild](#vertex-shaders-with-layer-entry-points-left-out-of-the-prebuild)). Before chain d403eb0683df the host found out when Metal refused the pipeline and named the reason, and built it again. Their passes are layered ([Layered passes for vertex shaders that write the layer](rendering.md#layered-passes-for-vertex-shaders-that-write-the-layer)).
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Legends: Z-A](../../dm/games/0100F43008C44000/README.md): two full-screen vertex shaders (`57246f9a04af8529`, `b29f08619f379a83`: one triangle per instance, layer = instance + offset, 20 pixel shaders read the layer).

### The precompile look-ahead restores the scissor

- **Symptom:** the picture bright green and white from some scene on, in runs with pipelines compiled on demand (prewarm lists off or short) and in none with `DM_NVN_PREDICT=0`.
- **Cause:** after a pipeline miss the replay looks ahead through the rest of the submission, replaying state only, to compile the next pipelines in the background (`pso_predict_rest`). It restored `S`, but `state_op` also sets the scissor, which lives in `R`: the replay went on with the last scissor of the submission until its next `OP_SCISSOR`, so the draws in between were clipped to another pass's rectangle.
- **Fix:** the scissor is saved and restored with `S`; the fusion probe (`fz_probe_cov2`) already restored all of `R`. Every game.
- **Found on:** [OCTOPATH TRAVELER II](../../dm/games/0100A3501946E000/README.md): from Castti's opening flashback and the battlefield of Hikari's opening on.
- **Check:** found by bisecting with four runs started at once on copies of one save: `DM_NVN_PREDICT=0` fixed it, a look-ahead that compiled nothing did not, and of its parts only the state ops brought it back; four runs green before the fix, four right after it.

### Pipeline look-ahead must leave future attachments unresolved

- **Symptom:** a crash in `-[AGXG16XFamilyRenderContext_mtlnext setRenderPipelineState:]` from `draw_` with direct Metal encoding. The pass dump shows a pipeline miss and an end of the open pass, from a wait for the GPU before a CPU upload, before the draw reaches its pipeline bind.
- **Cause:** `pso_predict_rest_` resolved the attachments of future `SetRenderTargets` records (`tex_mtl`) to get their formats and sample counts. That can make and upload a texture the replay has not reached; upstream's sparse texture read GPU-written storage for the upload, and the wait ended and committed the open pass, so the draw that missed bound its pipeline on an ended encoder. Deferred encoding can hide it. On NX 18 the look-ahead made and uploaded future attachments the same way (the fixture below); whether one of its upload paths ends the open pass as upstream's did is not established.
- **Fix:** the look-ahead reads only textures already made (`tex_made`) for formats and sample counts, skips a pass with an attachment not made yet, and goes on at the next pass whose attachments are known. The replay makes textures at their first use. This also keeps speculative uploads and GPU waits out of the look-ahead. Ported from upstream (`5012821ef`).
- **Found on:** [Mario Kart World](../../dm/games/0400C3F00006E000/README.md#found-on-this-game) 1.8.0 at start-up (upstream, M4 Pro, macOS 27.2.1). Not seen on NX 18.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_pending` sends the look-ahead through a pass with a target not made yet, then one already made. Before the fix it allocated a texture and queued a pipeline for the unknown attachment set too (2 compiles); with it, no allocation and only the known target's pipeline.

### The look-ahead hands every later draw's pipeline to the background

- **Symptom:** on entering a new area or opening a menu for the first time, with no archive or prewarm list covering it, frames of 0.2–2 s whose `[longframe]` line is nearly all pipeline work, and `draws that waited: pulling base not made yet` in the `pipelines up to frame` lines.
- **Cause:** after a submission's first pipeline miss, the look-ahead (`pso_predict_rest`) handed at most two of the rest to the background (`PSO_PREDICT_BUDGET`, `PSO_PEND_ACTIVE`), a bound set without a measurement. It applied to the keys of the draws the look-ahead met, with their attachments as they are, although those draws come later in the same submission. A draw with no stand-in whose pipeline was not admitted compiled it on the submit thread, so a frame with dozens of new pipelines compiled them one after another.
- **Fix:** each of those draws' pipelines is admitted (`pso_pend_kick_next`, bounded by the 384-slot table), after the same exclusions as before: a key whose base is made (`pso_base_made`), one that already drew with a stand-in (`g_pso_hy`) and one whose pulling stand-in is ready (`hybrid_get`) take nothing. The draw's own pipeline is built before the look-ahead runs, and a draw whose build is still queued makes it itself (`pso_pend_take`), so none waits behind the others. Host only, every game. Upstream starts every one of them at user-initiated QoS on the global queue. NX 18 starts the first `PSO_PREDICT_BUDGET` (2) there as before (the draws met first, then the guesses with other depth / stencil attachments, which keep that bound), and every further draw on `pp_bg_queue`'s serial utility-QoS queues (one per two performance cores, at least half of what Metal compiles at once, at most 3; `DM_PSO_BG_QUEUES`), the queues of the builds hybrid draws ask for: a burst on the global queue ran on every core beside the game's threads ([the hybrid builds' queue](#a-prebuild-pool-sized-from-the-super-cores)), and on the iPhone the battery is weighed with the frame rate. A draw that reaches its build while it waits there makes it itself, as before this change; one whose build a utility worker already runs waits for it with that worker raised to the draw's QoS until it ends (`pso_pend_take`, `pthread_override_qos_class_start_np`; a condition variable lends none), so it does not wait on a utility-QoS compile where it used to compile at its own QoS. These later draws' builds do not count toward `PSO_PEND_ACTIVE`, so a backlog of them on the utility queues (from a submission whose draws took their stand-ins instead) never sends the next look-ahead's first two there or refuses its guesses. `DM_NVN_PREDICT_NEXT=0` keeps the bound of two for them all (as before), `ab` alternates with `DM_AB`; kept in player builds, for the iPhone battery A/B. No player setting.
- **Found on:** upstream, [FINAL FANTASY RESONANCE](../../dm/games/0100606026F12000/README.md) (PR #1344, Apple M5 Max): a save loaded into Grandshelt Castle, then the System Menu and the World Map, with no archive, lists or prebuild and the system's Metal cache off: 2.78–2.81 s of pipeline waits, worst frame 2.19–2.23 s, before; 0.74–0.79 s, worst 0.71–0.74 s, after (two runs each). Bounding all of them by 16 instead gave 1.9–2.0 s. Repeated upstream on [OCTOPATH TRAVELER II](../../dm/games/0100A3501946E000/README.md) (Apple M4 Pro, `bench.py`, two runs each, cold the same way): draws that waited 200–205 for 5.0–6.0 s before, 120–128 for 3.0–3.3 s after; median frame time unchanged. Upstream's three-way A/B of one host: a fixed set of 10 workers taking the builds in the draws' order waited 2.61–2.64 s, 5 workers 2.78–2.95 s, against 2.50–2.53 s with every build started at once on the global queue; so NX 18's 1–3 utility queues may give back part of the gain on a Mac, and are not measured in NX 18 (neither game run here, nor the iPhone).
- **Check:** `dm/gfx/tests/t_pso_pending.m` (`test_next_draws_pass_the_guess_limit`, `test_prediction_admits_every_later_draw`, which also checks the QoS split, `test_next_backlog_keeps_first_two_on_global_queue`, `test_wait_on_running_next_build_lends_qos`; run it with `DM_NVN_PREDICT_NEXT=0` for the bound of two, and with `DM_PSO_BG_QUEUES=1`). Without `MTL_SHADER_CACHE_SIZE=0` a second run of the same route finds every pipeline in the system's cache and waits almost nothing either way, so cold comparisons need it, with `DM_PSO_ARCHIVE=off`, `DM_PSO_MANIFEST=off` and `DM_PSO_PREBUILD=0`. Open: an iPhone A/B (`DM_NVN_PREDICT_NEXT=ab`) on a cold area entry, for the waits, the long frames and the battery.

### GPU frame captures open no pipeline archive

- **Symptom:** a GPU frame capture (`DM_GPU_CAPTURE`, or Xcode with `MTL_CAPTURE_ENABLED=1`) fails with `Capturing Render pipeline specialization is not supported`, or the capture layer crashes on the pipeline archive; a capture of the `MTLDevice` is refused.
- **Cause:** at first, Metal could not capture pipelines made from Metal 4 bases, and its Metal 4 to 3 conversion for the capture layer crashed on the pipeline archive. A device the replay drives through Metal 4 cannot be the capture object. With Xcode 27.3 beta on macOS 27.3 (Apple M4 Max), measured for the Metal 4-only release, specialization is no longer the problem, but the archive still is. Pipelines `MTL4Compiler` builds whole, and pipelines specialized from a base (`newRenderPipelineStateBySpecializationWithDescriptor`), capture and replay, with their state, source and profile. `MTL4Archive` does not work with the capture layer loaded:
  - every archive lookup misses, for an archive that hits without the layer;
  - once the process has called `newArchiveWithURL`, Metal refuses to capture (`Capturing Metal 4 Device is not supported`), and so does the `gpucapture` tool;
  - any compile with `MTL4CompilerTaskOptions.lookupArchives` crashes (`SIGSEGV` in `Metal4To3ConversionUtility::convertBinaryArchives`), before any capture starts;
  - a pipeline serializer run under the layer writes an archive that a later normal run misses in for every entry.
- **Fix:** first, a run that might capture built its render pipelines through the `MTLDevice`, without bases or archive. That path is gone in the Metal 4-only release. Now such a run takes the *GPU capture profile* (`pso4_capture` in `nvn_mtl.m`). It opens no pipeline archive (neither the one bundled with the app nor `pso_archive/`), never calls `newArchiveWithURL`, makes no serializer and writes no archive file, and no run sets `lookupArchives`. It still builds every pipeline through the one `MTL4Compiler`, bases and specializations as in any run, so the captured pipelines are the ones a normal run draws with. With no archive the prebuild has nothing to build into. The profile is keyed on the capture layer itself, not on the variable that loads it: `MTLCaptureManager`'s `supportsDestination:` for a GPU trace document, asked after the device is made, says yes with `MTL_CAPTURE_ENABLED=1` or `METAL_CAPTURE_ENABLED=1` and in an iOS app with `MetalCaptureEnabled` in its `Info.plist` (a build with `DM_METAL_CAPTURE=1`, which loads the layer with no variable set, for Xcode's capture button), and no with `MTL_CAPTURE_ENABLED=0` or under `MTL_DEBUG_LAYER=1` (the validation layer loads in its place, and archives work). `DM_GPU_CAPTURE` (a capture needs the layer) and the `Info.plist` key give the profile too. The log says `GPU capture profile (<why>): no pipeline archive is opened or written; every pipeline is compiled through MTL4Compiler, whole or specialized from its base`. There is no `MTLDevice` pipeline to fall back to. `gpu_capture_frame` captures the Metal 4 queue (`g_q4`), which runs the frames, the uploads, the present and the host's [one-off command buffers](frame-rate.md#one-off-command-buffers-on-the-metal-4-queue) (readbacks, screenshots). Such a run compiles every pipeline it needs, so it starts more slowly than a normal one and its frame times are not the game's. Every game.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (Mac and iPhone).
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> check_t_pso_capture`: a run without the capture layer seeds an archive and a bundle, runs with `MTL_CAPTURE_ENABLED=1`, with `METAL_CAPTURE_ENABLED=1` and with `DM_GPU_CAPTURE` open neither, make no serializer and write nothing, yet build, specialize, draw and dispatch, and runs with `MTL_CAPTURE_ENABLED=0` and with none open both again; the `Info.plist` key alone gives the profile. `check_t_pso_depth_only` runs with the profile too.
- **Open:** a capture of a real game under the profile, on the Mac and on an iPhone, has not been made in this tree.

### A cold Metal cache looks like a long load

- **Symptom:** the first boot sat on the loading clock for about a minute, the CPU idle, with every file read done after 12 s.
- **Cause:** that run compiled its Metal libraries and pipelines on first use (cold cache). With a warm cache the title is up after about 15 s; what looked like a 40-second wait in the read log (`DM_FSLOG`) was the title screen itself, which reads nothing.
- **Check:** screenshots every few seconds (`ctl.sh snap` from a script) told the two apart.
- **Found on:** [Bayonetta 3](../../dm/games/01004A4010FEA000/README.md) (SDK 13.3.2).

### Native mesh draws omitted while their pipeline compiles

- **Symptom:** missing geometry or bright patches just after loading, which disappear as background pipeline compilation finishes.
- **Cause:** `mtk_pso` started an asynchronous native task/mesh pipeline build and returned no pipeline; the draw was omitted until a later frame found the completed pipeline. This copied the geometry-mesh optimization's behavior without its equivalent compute fallback. Native mesh draws have no such replacement. Metal validation also rejected the helper descriptor assigning a mesh function to `vertexFunction` before copying its raster state.
- **Fix:** compile the demanded native mesh pipeline before issuing its first draw, then reuse the cached pipeline. The shared descriptor helper leaves `vertexFunction` unset for a native mesh shader; the mesh descriptor receives it as `meshFunction`. A cold pipeline can delay the first frame but cannot silently remove its draw. `t_mesh_first_use` exercises real NVN submissions with mesh-only, task/mesh, depth-only and ordinary vertex shaders: the old host failed 10 of 42 pixel/state checks; the fixed host passes all 42 with Metal validation in both encoding modes.
- **Found on:** [Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md), M4 Pro startup white patches. The synthetic GPU regression verifies the missing-draw mechanism; the corrected game's startup remains to be checked with the queue fix.

### Geometry runs across occlusion query boundaries

- **Symptom:** hundreds of geometry raster passes in combat, despite support for batching consecutive geometry draws.
- **Cause:** the lookahead stopped at each samples-passed query report/reset. NieR wraps most geometry draws in independent occlusion queries, so each one still captured vertices, ran geometry/compaction and reopened the depth buffer.
- **Fix:** queries remain in their original raster replay positions while lookahead prepares later geometry draws. The shared raster pass uses a separate visibility-result slot after each report/reset; helper capture and compute stages do not count raster samples. Query-result addresses remain marked as report writes for hazard checks. `DM_NVN_GEOMETRY_QUERY_RUN=0` retains the earlier split path for comparison.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US 1.0.3](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md): the captured factory combat frame has 697 geometry raster draws with 670 intervening query/event/reset groups. A GPU fixture verifies that the first visible draw reports 216 samples and the second, hidden draw reports zero, with the completion event and later color/depth coverage preserved.

### Prepared geometry indices reused after byte comparison

- **Symptom:** geometry preparation dominates rendering CPU even after geometry passes have been batched; primitive assembly and capture-index deduplication repeat for unchanged meshes.
- **Cause:** each draw decodes all source indices, assembles its primitive map and rebuilds sorted unique capture IDs, even when its source bytes and topology are identical to the previous frame.
- **Fix:** a 32 MB / 4,096-entry LRU cache keeps only the CPU primitive map, unique capture IDs and original index bytes. The key includes address, count, index width, topology and restart settings. Every reuse compares all original bytes, after the existing readability and GPU-write wait checks; there is no content-hash collision assumption or dependence on write-protection. CPU edits, GPU copies and reused storage with different contents rebuild preparation. Base vertex, instances, vertex capture, transforms and GPU outputs remain per draw. `DM_NVN_GEOMETRY_INDEX_CACHE=0` disables reuse for comparison.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US 1.0.3](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md): the outdoor flight-unit encounter has approximately 2,300 draws including 855 geometry draws, 24.9 ms rendering CPU and 12.2 ms GPU time. A controlled CPU-only preparation benchmark (64 draws, 32 meshes, 6,144 indices each), alternating warmed paths, drops median CPU preparation from 2.783 to 0.149 ms; it does not execute the GPU geometry stages or establish a gameplay FPS. Production GPU tests preserve literal pixel coverage on unchanged repeats, CPU index edits and GPU index copies, including forced-wait and disabled-cache paths. Cache budget and topology/restart separation are covered too.

### Geometry capture initializes only missing words

- **Symptom:** geometry vertex-capture passes consume substantial GPU time before geometry compute or depth rasterization.
- **Cause:** the capture entry first zeroed all 144 words of every output record, then wrote the default W components and finally overwrote the actual shader outputs. Much of the initialization was redundant GPU storage traffic.
- **Fix:** `dmsh_geometry.capture` initializes only words absent from the captured outputs. The capture slot map stores each selected word once, including defaults for absent outputs. Position W, point size, generic W, clip distances, layer and viewport retain their existing bit values. Full capture selects all 144 words; lean capture selects its consumed words. Shaders and host must be rebuilt together.
- **Check:** the synthetic throughput comparison alternates control/candidate order and verifies all 9,437,184 captured words against both the original buffer and literal expected values. Mac runs vary in timing, so their percentage gain does not establish iPhone gameplay FPS. The optional real-translator capture fixture in `t_geometry` also checks all uncaptured defaults. All 1,225 NieR shaders build for Mac and iPhone; standard geometry replay and dependency checks pass.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata US 1.0.3](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md#factory-combat-capture-and-9s-texture-follow-up), iPhone City Ruins: 14 geometry groups in the captured frame spend 7.485 ms in vertex capture, 6.512 ms in compute and 1.019 ms in depth raster. The accompanying pass dump affects total frame time; its large uncovered GPU interval must not be treated as a clean gameplay hitch measurement.

### The host's own shaders compiled from source at run time

- **Symptom:** Apple's performance HUD (`DM_METAL_HUD=1`) says `Compiled 2 shaders. Total compilation time is 102.70ms. Consider adopting MTL4Compiler for more fine grained compilations` during play, with a dip in the frame-rate graph at that moment, for example when a new room first issues a scissored clear.
- **Cause:** the host compiled the shaders it writes itself from MSL source at run time (`newLibraryWithSource:`): built-in kernels, clears, copies, `nvnCommandBufferDrawTexture`, the RGB565 red / blue exchange, index conversions, geometry packing, transform feedback, the present, the GPU deswizzle and the OpenGL layer's clear quad. The thread that asked waits for the compiler. The clear triangles were one library per colour slot and type (`snprintf`), up to 24, each compiled when a clear of that kind was first met: about 50 ms each on an iPhone, on the submitting thread. `MTL4Compiler`'s `newLibraryWithDescriptor:` with a source is no way around it: it calls the device's `newLibraryWithSource:` itself (a check with a swizzled device shows the call), and the HUD counts that too.
- **Fix:** the sources are `.metal` files in `dm/gfx/msl` (the clears in one library: `clr_fs_<slot>_<float|uint|int>`). `dm/gfx/tools/host_msl.py` compiles them when the host is built: `make -C dm/host` for the Mac, and `gen_build.py` with `MSL_SDK=iphoneos` for the app's deployment target on iOS. It writes `dm/host/gen/msl-<sdk>/host_msl_gen.h`, which holds each library's metallib and source. `host_msl_lib` (`dm/gfx/nvn/host_msl.m`) loads one by name with `newLibraryWithData:`, once per device, so nothing is compiled. The host's pipelines are built whole through the host's one `MTL4Compiler` (`g_comp4`), the present and CMAA2 included: `host_msl_cps` and `host_msl_rps` (`host_msl.m`, called through `host_cps` / `host_rps` in `nvn_mtl.m`) make a Metal 4 descriptor from a library and function names, with constant values when there are some. A host draw with no fragment (depth or stencil only) takes the empty fragment `dm_nop` (`nop_frag.metal`), with every write mask none, because Metal 4 needs a fragment whenever rasterization is on; the game's depth-only pipelines take the same one. At first a build without the Metal toolchain carried the sources only, and the host compiled each library once at run time with a `[RED]` line. Since the Metal 4-only release a build without the Metal toolchain stops with an error (`host_msl.py: no Metal compiler for <sdk>: install the selected Xcode's Metal toolchain …`): there is no sources-only header and no run-time compile. The header keeps the sources only for `t_geometry`, which extends one; the host reads none. The empty fragment function that a native mesh pipeline with no pixel shader takes (`mesh_nop.metal`, `mtk_nop_fragment` in `nvn_geometry.inc`) was compiled from source too, on the thread that built the first such pipeline, and is a library of its own as well. The first load logs `host shader libraries: 19, compiled for <sdk> by <metal version>`.
